If Q27 folds Phase 9's per-item bucket column (LIMITE L5, the pending TIMER_SET defect) into migration M, the kind-5 item stores the same value there and `SEND` reads it. Tested by the timer-change world case (§15.4), JM4 and mutant PM-M25.

### 5.5 The media lane in `:sync` (normative; the "media bulk lane" of F7:1434)

| Change | Rule |
|---|---|
| Consumer priority (stores) | inside every store round (a STANDARD pass or a HIGH pair event), due ops are taken `DM, PREKEYS, CHANNEL, IDENTITY` before `MEDIA`, then by `not_before_minute` and `operation_id` (F7 had no fairness) |
| Media store budget | `mediaStoresPerPass = 16` per STANDARD pass, taken **after** the pass's `storesPerPass = 32` non-media stores, the pass reposting itself while media is due (`Session.kt:178`'s at-cap rule) so other work items interleave between passes; `mediaStoresPerPairEvent = 8` in HIGH |
| Fetch fairness (new) | the work lane has **one thread** (`Session.threads`, `Session.kt:129`) and fetches run inside `PairWork` items on it (`WorkLane.kt:77-83`), so a long media bundle delays every DM fetch behind it. A media pair's `PairWork` therefore fetches at most `mediaFetchesPerItem = 8` blobs and reposts the rest of its bundle as a new item **behind** the non-media items already queued; a non-media `PairWork` is never queued behind a media continuation |
| Media fetch budget | pairs of a `Consumer.MEDIA` namespace fetch up to `mediaForegroundFetchesPerEvent = 64` per event and `mediaBackgroundFetchesPerEvent = 128` per background event, in slices of 8 |
| Media share of the relay budget (new) | in a background session media work (stores and fetches) may use at most `mediaBackgroundRelayWorkMillis = 45 000` of each relay's 90 s work budget (`TrafficPolicy.backgroundRelayWorkMillis`, charged by `Steps.timed`, `Steps.kt:91`); the rest stays for DM, bundle and channel work of that relay |
| Unchanged | the read schedule (T19), list limits and pages, `FETCHED_CAP = 256` per namespace (the media actor drains `M` continuously and never stops claiming, §7.5), quorum 2, the 7-day window, deadlines, resends |

T19 holds by construction: budgets and slicing change how many `get`s and stores a work item carries and in which order queued items run, never when lists happen. `T19BasicTest` gains media activity in its run (b), and mutant MC-M14 (media work moves a list) must be detected. A new `MediaFairnessTest` measures, in a world where a card holder floods `M_0`, the delay of DM fetches against a control without media: at most one media slice (8 calls) per DM fetch. The Phase 7 exhaustive suite and `sync-exit-gate` re-run with a `MEDIA` consumer in their worlds.

**Throughput (estimates, [I]; measured in S0b, V-M10).** Onion-service bulk throughput was a median 2.5–5.4 Mbit/s over 2026-09-10 … 20 (Tor Metrics onionperf), but Phase 7 builds a new relay client per RPC (pooling is a Phase 12 item, F7:85), so one 64 KiB store or `get` costs about two round trips plus the transfer, 0.7–1.6 s. **The work lane is serial across relays**, so an upload costs `3 × (F + 1)` calls one after another: an image (51 store calls) about 36–82 s in the foreground; a class-8 object (1 299 calls) about 15–35 min; a class-10 video (2 415 calls) about 28–64 min in the foreground, and in background jobs about 18–40 jobs per relay (a job gives media at most 45 s per relay, about 28–64 calls). A download is `F + 1` calls: 12–27 s for an image, 9–21 min for class 10. Upload is three replicas: 3.2 MiB for an image, 151 MiB for a 50 MiB video, plus about 3 % of Tor cell overhead (498 payload bytes per 514-byte cell). **Worked example:** a 25 000 000-byte video is class 8 (432 fragments, capacity 28 309 824 B), so 433 blobs and 28 377 088 B per relay (+13.5 %), 85.1 MB uploaded to three relays, 10.6 % of one weekly media allowance per relay, and about 15–35 min of foreground upload at the per-RPC cost above. **No transfer-time claim is made** (finding F-M2: connection pooling is the lever).

### 5.6 `MediaDestination` and the channel contract (Phase 10)

The engine (§6, §7) never names a DM concept; it receives a destination:

```kotlin
class MediaDestination(
    val destId: DestinationId,           // 16 B: the contact id for a DM; in key_env's binding (§4.2)
    val namespace: NamespaceId,          // M_c for a DM; the channel's media namespace in Phase 10
    val relays: Set<RelayId>,            // the namespace's relay set
    val ttl: TtlBucket,                  // the conversation's or channel's bucket (R-MT1)
    val deadlineEpochSeconds: Long,      // author + min(bucket, 7 d) (R-MT2)
)
```

Phase 10 must decide, and ADR-45 lists as requirements on it: a relay-blind channel media namespace with its read and write credentials; automatic download by members (the T28 argument of §7.1 applies unchanged) and the relay egress of fan-out (a 50 MiB post read by 1 000 members is about 50 GB leaving the relays; finding F-M5); the key slot in MLS application messages (§4.3); the channel timer mapped by R-MT1; and what a removed member keeps (the keys of objects it already received). Phase 11's gate closes on DMs and on a test destination in the JVM world (§15.5); FR-3.4's channel acceptance belongs to Phase 10.

---

## 6. Sender pipeline (normative; ADR-45, ADR-46)

**End to end: A sends a photo to B, who is offline** (each step is specified below and in §7).
1. A picks a photo; `sendMedia` returns `QUEUED`. A fresh sandbox process re-encodes it to a canonical JPEG of at most 1 048 512 B and dies; Rust canonicalises the main process's copy; a sealer seals 4 chunks into 16 fragment files and builds a 64 KiB manifest (§6.2).
2. `MEDIA-COMMIT` stores the object, `key_env`, the message row (its countdown not yet started, D26) and the pending item in one transaction (§6.3).
3. `MEDIA-FEED` enqueues the manifest and 16 fragments into `M_B` with B's shared media-deposit credential, the conversation's TTL and the op deadline; Phase 7 stores each on the three relays of `set(M_B, w)` and verifies two operators' copies (§6.4).
4. After the last outcome, `MEDIA-READY` starts the sender's countdown and lets F9's `SEND` encrypt the kind-5 L0 (the key slot filled inside Rust) and deposit a 4 KiB envelope into `X_B`.
5. B comes online. Phase 7 lists `X_B` and `M_B` on their own schedules and fetches everything new, independently of each other (§7.1).
6. `MEDIA-INGEST` stores the 17 blobs as ciphertext files; `RECV` opens the descriptor (through `decrypt` or, for a first message, `sessionInbound`) and records the object with a fresh `key_env`; `MEDIA-BIND` opens the manifest and binds the fragments by hash; `MEDIA-VERIFY` authenticates and validates every chunk (§7.2, §7.3).
7. When B opens the conversation, the preview is decoded in the sandbox; opening the photo decrypts it into shared memory for the sandbox to decode (§7.4). No step from 5 on causes relay traffic beyond `M_B`'s scheduled listings and fetches.
8. At the message's expiry both devices delete the object's rows and key in the SWEEP transaction, checkpoint the WAL, and delete its files after the commit; the relays drop the blobs by their bucket (§11.1).

### 6.1 Sources

- **Picker only.** `ActivityResultContracts.PickVisualMedia` (the system photo picker on API 30+ with Modular System Components; on API 29 it falls back to `ACTION_OPEN_DOCUMENT`, developer.android.com photo-picker page). No storage permission is requested (none is in T15). The GMS backport's `com.google.android.gms.metadata.ModuleDependencies` manifest entry is forbidden (ADR-06; G-M4). What the picker and, on API 29, DocumentsUI record about a pick is written by the OS, not by GHOST; **measured in S0 (V-M21, S0-17)** it is more than "recent documents": thumbnails of the pictures shown (in shared storage on both levels; on API 29 of every picture the picker displays), a zero-filled placeholder named after the picked item (API 35), GHOST's package name with the folder and time of its last pick and a per-file pick counter (API 29), all surviving reboots; L9-M15 lists them and Q42 asks whether to warn or refuse. The photo picker hands the picture over with its GPS fields zeroed; DocumentsUI hands over the file unchanged; STRIP removes both. On an API 29 or API 30 phone **with** Play services, `PickVisualMedia` may open the Play services photo picker instead of DocumentsUI, whose residues are an S0b checklist line.
- **No copy.** The content URI is read with `ContentResolver.openInputStream` straight into a `SharedMemory` region (images, ≤ 64 MiB) or handed to Transformer through Media3's `ContentDataSource` (video); nothing is copied to `cacheDir` or any file (S0 V-M11: reading the picked stream wrote nothing into the app's directories on API 29 and 35). On API 29 DocumentsUI returns a **read and write** grant (`0x43`) on the user's own file; GHOST opens it read-only (`openInputStream`, never `openOutputStream` or a write mode) and never calls `takePersistableUriPermission` (G-M1; the photo picker grants read only, `0x41`; S0-7).
- **Image sources accepted:** JPEG, PNG and static WebP. **HEIC/HEIF and AVIF are refused before decoding** (`sniff` → `UNSUPPORTED`, `sendMedia` → `UNSUPPORTED_SOURCE`): the platform decodes them through the media server, which an isolated process may not reach (S0 V-M5: `servicemanager` refuses `media.player` to isolated apps; API 29 blocks until the request's timeout, API 35 fails at once; API 29 has no AVIF decoder at all), and decoding them in the main process is what D17 forbids; Q39 asks whether to allow a main-process decode through the media server for these two formats only (S0-4). Refused before decoding (magic-byte sniff in Rust): GIF, animated WebP, DNG and other RAW, BMP, ICO, WBMP, SVG, anything else. **Video sources:** ISO-BMFF files that `Mp4Extractor` parses (MP4, 3GP, QuickTime) with H.264, H.265, VP9 or AV1 video that the device can decode; `MediaNative.sniff` accepts a video source only with an `ftyp` whose brands are not HEIF or AVIF image brands (`heic`, `heix`, `mif1`, `msf1`, `avif`, `avis`), and Transformer parses it with `Mp4Extractor` alone (S0-21), so every other container is refused with a typed error (S0, API 29 and 35: PNG, JPEG, WebP, HEIF, AVIF and WebM picked as "video" all failed; with Media3's default extractors the WebM file was transcoded, so revision 3's "refused by Transformer's error" did not hold).
- **Limits before work:** image source ≤ 64 MiB and ≤ 100 megapixels (header), video source duration ≤ `VIDEO_MAX_MS` = 160 000, or ≤ `VIDEO_MAX_MS_SHORT` = 85 000 in a conversation whose bucket is 1 h (class 8, R-MT2), each checked before a byte is re-encoded. The two durations are the byte budget (50 MiB, or class 8's 28 309 824 B) divided by 1.2 × the profile's 2.128 Mbit/s (164.2 s and 88.7 s), rounded down to a multiple of 5 s (revision 2's 190 000 and 100 000 left 3.7 % and 6.4 % of headroom). **They bound the source's duration only, not the output's size (S0 V-M22, S0-18):** on hard content the emulators' software encoders overshot 2 Mbit/s by +29 % to +169 % (a noise patch on moving content) and by up to +4 232 % on full-frame noise (a synthetic upper bound), because their rate control stops at its quantiser cap (`DEFAULT_QP_MAX` 40 in AOSP's `SoftAVCEnc` and `C2SoftAvcEnc`; Media3 sets no quantiser bound), so no fixed margin holds and revision 3's "the margin is fixed before S5" is withdrawn. **The class cap is enforced while writing instead (§6.2):** an output that reaches it is cancelled at once, retried once at 15 fps when that can fit, and otherwise refused (`TOO_LARGE`, or `TOO_LARGE_FOR_TIMER` under R-MT2). The encoder is asked for VBR (Media3's default; revision 3 asked for CBR where supported, but on API 35's encoder CBR overshot more than VBR on every hard source, and API 29's has no CBR); the phones' encoders are measured in both modes in S0b, and CBR is used only on an encoder measured better in it (Q41).

### 6.2 Preparation (no database transaction; the media worker thread)

**Image:** the sandbox's `STRIP_IMAGE` (§9.2), in a process instance of its own, decodes, orients, converts to sRGB, drops any gain map, scales to the canonical geometry of D29 (long edge 512, 1 024 or 2 048 px, the largest not above the source's, and 512 for smaller sources; short edge rounded to a multiple of 16) and re-encodes baseline JPEG with adaptive quality until the output is ≤ `IMAGE_MAX_CONTENT`; after the instance is torn down, the main process copies the output out of the shared region (so the bytes it checks are the bytes it seals, and no live sandbox can change them), runs `MediaNative.jpegCanonicalize` (§8.2) on its copy, builds a preview with a 320-px long edge the same way (unless view-once), and feeds the canonical bytes to a sealer.

**Video:** Transformer transcodes to the profile of §8.3 through `GhostMuxer`, whose channel is a `SealingChannel` that hands every completed 262 128-byte chunk of plaintext to `MediaNative.sealAppend` and writes the four returned fragments as ciphertext files (§10.4). The sealer's fMP4 walker validates the stream as it passes and builds the `mfra` index, which `sealFinish` appends (§8.3). `sealFinish` receives the preview and takes the output's dimensions and duration from its own walker (§3.5).
- **The preview comes from the canonical output, not from the source (S0-19, S0 review I15).** `GhostMuxer` keeps a copy of the first video sample (a sync sample: every fragment starts with one) and the track's `avcC` parameter sets; after the export completes, `GhostTranscoder` decodes that one sample with a `MediaCodec` H.264 decoder into a `ByteBuffer` (not a surface), converts it to RGB with the output's colour label (limited range, the matrix of §8.3 point 5(d), S0-13) and turns it by the output's `tkhd` rotation (S0-20); the main process then scales its own pixels to the 320-px long edge, compresses them and canonicalises the result (§8.2). **Verified in S0 (V-M23, 2026-10-04):** for the colour-grid sources in every rotation (0°, 90°, 180°, 270°, coded portrait, no audio) the grid came out in place on API 35 and API 29, every patch within 2–4 levels per channel of its source colour when converted with the matrix the encoder really coded (BT.709 or BT.601 on API 35 as requested, BT.601 on API 29 whatever was requested), and no file was written. Revision 3 took the preview from the source, through a second ExoPlayer into an `ImageReader` surface: that parsed the untrusted source a second time, created a second platform playback session (V-M13), and returned frames in the coded orientation and in a luma range that differed between the two emulators (V-M23), so the conversion would have needed a per-device range table built from emulator behaviour. The output path has none of these: its codec, range and rotation are fixed by D29, and the decode runs in the main process's `MediaCodec` client as playback does (§9.1), over GHOST's own canonical bytes. If the decode fails, the object carries **no preview** (`preview_len = 0`, the UI shows a placeholder; the preview is inside the encryption, so relays see no difference). Rendering from the sealed object instead would need the manifest that the preview is part of.
- **Encoder names.** Before `sealFinish`, `GhostTranscoder` requires Transformer's `ExportResult.videoEncoderName` and `audioEncoderName` to be non-null (each is null when no encoder ran for its track, i.e. when Transformer transmuxed it; with the forced silent track every output has audio, §8.3); otherwise it calls `sealAbort` and the preparation fails (`UNSUPPORTED_SOURCE`, detail `NOT_ENCODED`, counted; §8.3).
- **The class cap while sealing, and one retry (S0-18, Q41).** `GhostMuxer` counts the bytes of the samples it is given plus the container's boxes and fails the export with `OVERSIZE` as soon as they exceed the largest class the send may use (class 10, or class 8 under R-MT2), recording at that moment the projection `bytes × source duration ÷ media time written`. The projection decides the retry and nothing else: if it is at most twice the cap, the preparation runs **once more with `FrameDropEffect` at 15 fps** (and the encoder told 15 fps, §8.3 point 5); otherwise, or when the second run also reaches the cap, it fails with `TOO_LARGE` (`TOO_LARGE_FOR_TIMER` under R-MT2). Every run's fragments are discarded with `sealAbort` before the next. **Why fewer frames and not a lower bitrate (S0 V-M22, review I5):** on the API 35 emulator a lower requested bitrate did not shrink the output at all in VBR (2.0, 1.0 and 0.5 Mbit/s gave byte-identical files on smooth, mixed and noise sources, with or without GHOST's other settings) and in CBR only down to a floor (1.29 Mbit/s on smooth content, nothing on hard content), because the rate control already sat at its quantiser cap; halving the frame rate cut hard content by 20–48 % (API 35) and by 44 % (API 29, whose VBR, its only mode, also stopped at a floor: 1.26 Mbit/s on smooth content and no reduction at all on the `mixed` source). The retried video plays at 15 fps, which tells the recipient that the first attempt was too large for its class (L9-M9). Whether a phone's hardware encoder honours a lower bitrate is an S0b line; Q41 holds the alternatives.
- **Stall watchdog.** An export whose progress (`Transformer.getProgress`) has not advanced for `EXPORT_STALL_MS` = 30 000 is cancelled (`UNSUPPORTED_SOURCE`, detail `STALLED`; S0: one 160-s export on the API 35 emulator stopped without an error, R-M19).
- **Typed failures.** A `VideoFrameProcessingException` from the GL stack maps to `UNSUPPORTED_SOURCE` with the detail `GL_UNAVAILABLE` (S0-1: an API 29 emulator without OpenGL ES 3 failed every export with `eglChooseConfig failed`; images are unaffected, and whether any supported phone lacks ES 3.0 is an S0b line); an encoder that fails to configure or start maps to `ENCODER_FAILED` (seen twice on the API 35 emulator: `configure()` on an already released encoder, R-M19); Transformer's container and decoder errors to `CONTAINER` and `CODEC`; more than eight input channels to `CHANNELS`; the fragment bound of §8.3 to `FRAGMENT_TOO_LARGE`; an encoder whose colour calibration matches neither label to `COLOUR_UNVERIFIED` (§8.3 point 5(d)). Every outcome after `sendMedia` returned `QUEUED` reaches the caller through `preparationState` (§6.5, S0-23).
- `MediaMetadataRetriever` and Media3's `FrameExtractor` are not used (G-M3).

**Files during preparation** get random names, **reserved in the media actor's in-memory `preparing` set before the first byte is written** (the temp file is named after the final name, so one reservation covers both) and released after `MEDIA-COMMIT`, so a concurrent reconcile keeps them (§10.4; §22.5 row 25: the first version of `CiphertextFiles` generated the name inside `write` and could not). An abort (`sealAbort`, a Transformer error, a size overflow, a process death) leaves orphans that the next reconcile deletes; the key never existed outside native memory (§3.7).

### 6.3 `MEDIA-COMMIT` (one transaction on the DM actor; `SEND`'s media half)

After `sealFinish` returned the manifest blob, its hash, `key_env`, the class and the hashes of the `F` fragments, and after every file was `force(true)`d and the directory synced:

1. Register `M_c` with `Namespaces.register(M_c, MEDIA, set(M_c, w), listen = false, LOCAL)` and install the `MD_{c,s}` credentials if absent (the F9 `EST-READY` pattern for `X_c`); delete a pending `dm_orphan_namespace(M_c)` row if one exists (§5.1).
2. Insert the `dm_message` (direction `out`, kind 5, `body_env` = the caption, state `queued`) and the `dm_outbound_item` (kind 5, `l0_env` = the L0 with a zero key slot, state `pending`), exactly as F9 `SEND` does for a text **except that no ratchet step and no enqueue of the descriptor happen yet, and the sender countdown does not start** (D26): `expire_at_minute` is inserted as the upper bound `ceil_minute(deadline_hour + timer)` and lowered to `ceil_minute(end + timer)` when the upload ends, at `MEDIA-READY` or, for an object that never became ready, at the op deadline (a FAILED object may still be retried until then), an "only earlier" update that `dm_message_expiry` already admits (R-T6, `Schema.kt:1179-1185`).
3. Insert `media_object(object_id, role = 'out', dest_id = c, kind, view_once, class_code, manifest_hash, key_env, state = 'uploading', message_id)`, one `media_blob(hash, file_name, origin = 'sealed')` per blob, and the `media_fragment` rows (`state = 'bound'`, `upload_state = 'unsent'`, ordinal −1 for the manifest and 0 … F − 1).
4. Insert `media_upload(object_id, namespace = M_c, ttl, deadline_hour, retry_gen = 0)`.
5. After the commit, remove the object's names from `preparing` and post `MEDIA-FEED`.

The order follows the foreign keys (`media_upload → sync_namespace`, `media_object → dm_message`). If the contact has no usable session yet, the item waits exactly as a text does (F9 §5.5), and the upload proceeds meanwhile: `M_c` needs no session. A crash before the commit loses the preparation (orphans reconciled, no key anywhere); a crash after it resumes at `MEDIA-FEED`.

**Why the countdown waits for the upload (D26, Q26).** With the countdown from the commit (revision 1), a 5-minute message whose upload takes longer than 5 minutes expired mid-burst: the feeder stopped and relays saw fewer than `F + 1` stores cut off about 5 minutes after they began, with no descriptor after them, which a 1-hour timer in the same TTL bucket never produces. That separated 5-minute from 1-hour timers, which R-T1 (ADR-27 point 11) merges precisely so relays cannot tell them apart. Starting the countdown at the upload's end makes the burst's length a function of the class, the deadline (itself a function of the bucket) and the network only. The cost: the sender's caption and ciphertext live up to the op deadline plus the timer (for a 5-minute timer, at most about 1 h 5 min after the commit), declared in L9-M13.

### 6.4 Upload: `MEDIA-FEED`, outcomes, `MEDIA-READY`

State lives **per ordinal**, in `media_fragment.upload_state` (`unsent`, `open`, `sent`, `degraded`, `indeterminate`, `failed`), and **per op**, in `media_outbound_op(operation_id → object_id, ordinal, retry_gen, resend_count)`: Phase 7's `OutboundOutcome` names only an operation id and a namespace (`sync/api/Outbox.kt`), so the op table is what maps an outcome back to its blob, exactly as F9's `dm_outbound_op` does for DM items (`Schema.kt:1017-1027`). `media_upload.retry_gen` counts the user's retries of the object (0 … 3).

```
op_id = SHA-256("ghost/v1/media/op" || object_id(16) || i16_be(ordinal) || u8(retry_gen) || u8(resend_count))[0..16]
```

- **`MEDIA-FEED`** (DM actor; triggered by `MEDIA-COMMIT`, by `SyncChange.OUTCOMES` for `Consumer.MEDIA`, by a retry and by time-triggered passes; **never by an inbound blob**, T28): while the object has fewer than 32 ordinals `open`, the device has fewer than 64, and an ordinal is `unsent` (lowest first, the manifest first), read that blob from its file, verify its SHA-256 against the row, and in one transaction `Outbox.enqueue(OutboundBlob(op_id, M_c, bytes, ttl, deadline))` with `resend_count = 0`, insert its `media_outbound_op` row and set the ordinal `open`. Deterministic op ids make a repeated feed idempotent (`AlreadyEnqueued`). At most 64 × 64 KiB = 4 MiB of media payload sits in `outbox_op` at any time (D13). A file whose hash does not match its row (local corruption) makes the object `failed` with no retry (the ciphertext is gone; `retryMedia` answers `NOT_RETRYABLE` and the user must pick the file again).
- **`MEDIA-OUTCOME`.** For each released outcome of a media op (`Outbox.outcomes(Consumer.MEDIA)` then `release`, F7 §9), look the op up: an op whose row is gone (its message was deleted by the user) is released and ignored (counted). Otherwise: SENT or DEGRADED → the ordinal's final state; INDETERMINATE with `resend_count < 2`, before `resendNotAfter` and the deadline → the Phase 7 identical-bytes resend as a new op (`resend_count + 1`, same `retry_gen`; F9 §7.5's rule), the ordinal stays `open`; INDETERMINATE otherwise → final `indeterminate`; FAILED → the ordinal `failed`, the object `failed`, the feeder stops, the message becomes `failed`; its countdown still waits (a retry may follow until the deadline, and a retried burst must not be cut by the timer either, D26).
- **`MEDIA-READY`** (one transaction, when no ordinal is `unsent`, `open` or `failed`, i.e. all `F + 1` are final in {`sent`, `degraded`, `indeterminate`}): `state = 'uploaded'`; the sender countdown starts (`expire_at_minute` lowered, §6.3); the `dm_outbound_item` becomes sendable, and F9's `SEND` runs for it in the same actor pass (session selection, the key-slot `encrypt` of §4.2, seal, `Outbox.enqueue` into `X_c` with the object's TTL and deadline from `media_upload`, never `ttlFor` of the conversation's current timer, R-MT4). The descriptor is a user send that completes the user's action, so it follows G-DM2's caller list (extended, §16.1).
- **Deadline.** At `deadline_hour` an object still `uploading` or `failed` is (or stays) `failed` (the ops close in Phase 7 by the same deadline) and the message's countdown starts; `retryMedia` then answers `DEADLINE_PASSED`. A process that is not running at the deadline applies this at its next start; the inserted upper bound `deadline_hour + timer` bounds the row meanwhile.
- **Why a final INDETERMINATE counts.** A copy may exist on the relay, and Phase 7 cannot tell; blocking the descriptor would lose a message that is probably complete, while sending it risks a recipient-side `incomplete` state (§7.5), which the recipient sees and which the sender's UI can report as "may be incomplete". FAILED means no copy exists and blocks.
- **User retry of a FAILED object** before the deadline: `retry_gen + 1` (at most 3), the object back to `uploading`, the `failed` ordinals back to `unsent`; `sent`, `degraded` and `indeterminate` ordinals are **not** stored again (CS-M4), and the ordinals are fed from the **same ciphertext files** (no re-encryption; FAILED means no copy existed, F7 §9). The new op ids differ by `retry_gen`. After the deadline the facade answers `DEADLINE_PASSED` and the user must pick the file again (a new object).
- **Descriptor outcomes** follow F9 `OUTCOME`. For a view-once object the sender deletes its fragment rows, files and `key_env` (in one transaction, then the checkpoint of §11.1 and a reconcile) once the descriptor op has a **final** outcome other than FAILED: SENT, DEGRADED, or INDETERMINATE after its resends (INDETERMINATE has no later path that would need the copy); a FAILED descriptor keeps them for a retry until the deadline, then the same cleanup runs. For a normal object they stay until the message expires (§11.1).
- **Timing visible to relays.** The descriptor deposit into `X_c` follows the last media outcome, so AD-1 sees a burst into `M_c` and, shortly after, a 4 KiB deposit into `X_c` from the same client session (L9-M2). HIGH mode's U[0, 10 min] delay keeps both inside one session and so does not break that link; only a descriptor deposited in a later session would, which Phase 11 does not do (Phase 12's decoupling, L7-D2).

### 6.5 Facade additions (`:messaging`, UI-less, for Phase 13)

```kotlin
fun sendMedia(contact: ContactId, source: MediaSource, caption: String?, viewOnce: Boolean): SendResult
    // QUEUED(preparation: PreparationId) | TOO_LARGE | TOO_LARGE_FOR_TIMER | UNSUPPORTED_SOURCE | KEY_CHANGED | BLOCKED
    // | REVOKED | REQUEST_NOT_ACCEPTED | NO_REPLICAS | LIMIT_REACHED | STORAGE_FULL | CRYPTO_UNAVAILABLE
    // (checks made before any work: sniff, header limits, contact state; retryMedia may also answer DEADLINE_PASSED or NOT_RETRYABLE)
fun preparationState(preparation: PreparationId): PreparationView?
    // PREPARING(progress) | COMMITTED(message: MessageId) | FAILED(reason: PrepFailure); null for an unknown id, after
    // the final state was read once, or after a process death (the preparation is gone, its orphans reconciled)
    // PrepFailure: UNSUPPORTED_SOURCE(detail) | UNDECODABLE | TOO_LARGE | TOO_LARGE_FOR_TIMER | STORAGE_FULL | CANCELLED
    // detail: CONTAINER | CODEC | CHANNELS | GL_UNAVAILABLE | FRAGMENT_TOO_LARGE | ENCODER_FAILED | STALLED | NOT_ENCODED
    //         | COLOUR_UNVERIFIED
fun mediaState(message: MessageId): MediaStateView?   // kind, class, state, progress counts (fragments held / F); no times
fun openMedia(message: MessageId): MediaHandle?       // a reader over a verified object (§10.2); null otherwise
fun openViewOnce(message: MessageId): MediaHandle?    // §11.2; null if already viewed, not verified or not view-once
fun retryMedia(message: MessageId): SendResult        // FAILED objects only, before the deadline, at most 3 times
fun setConversationOnScreen(contact: ContactId?)      // the UI's statement of which conversation is visible (null: none)
fun mediaPreview(message: MessageId): MediaPreview?   // decoded pixels from the RAM cache or the sandbox; null unless §7.4 allows
```

`MediaSource` wraps a content URI and nothing else. Every value type's `toString()` is redacted (T3). **Preparation outcomes (S0-23, S0 review I6).** Revision 3 had no way to report a failure that happens after `sendMedia` returned `QUEUED`, yet everything S0 added fails there: the GL stack (`GL_UNAVAILABLE`, S0-1), the fragment bound (`FRAGMENT_TOO_LARGE`, §8.3), `TOO_LARGE` after the retry rule (§6.2), the stall watchdog (`STALLED`), an encoder that fails at configure (`ENCODER_FAILED`, seen twice on the API 35 emulator) an export without encoder names (`NOT_ENCODED`, §8.3) or a failed colour calibration (`COLOUR_UNVERIFIED`, §8.3), and the sandbox's `UNDECODABLE`. `QUEUED` therefore carries a `PreparationId` (random, local), and `preparationState` reports the preparation until its final state has been read once: `COMMITTED` with the message id after `MEDIA-COMMIT`, or `FAILED` with a typed reason. The registry lives in the media worker's memory only: a preparation writes no row before `MEDIA-COMMIT` (no history of attempted sends, T33), and a process death loses it with its orphans (the UI then shows the item as not sent). Every reason is a value of `PrepFailure`, counted locally, and `MediaResultCodesTest` is exhaustive over `SendResult`, `PrepFailure` and its details. The facade's change listener fires on every state change (a local signal, no traffic, T47). A `MediaHandle` is closed by the UI; closing zeroises the native reader. `mediaState` of a kind-5 message received in a request answers `NOT_KEPT` (D28). **The on-screen signal.** Revision 2 made previews depend on "the conversation is on screen" (§7.4, §9.3) without any way for the engine to know it. `setConversationOnScreen` is that signal: `mediaPreview` decodes only for messages of the contact last named, only while the process is in the foreground (`ProcessLifecycleOwner` at `ON_START`, the signal `GhostApp` already observes), and never for a request or a blocked contact; `ON_STOP`, lock and `null` clear it. It is a local call with no traffic (T47), and the gate harness uses it (§15.8), since Phase 13's screens do not exist yet.

### 6.6 Limits

`MAX_CONCURRENT_PREPARATIONS = 2`, `MEDIA_WINDOW_PER_OBJECT = 32`, `MEDIA_WINDOW = 64`, `MAX_RESENDS = 2` per ordinal and retry generation, `MAX_USER_RETRIES = 3` per object, `MAX_DECODER_PROCESSES = 2` live at once (a pre-bound spare counts, §9.2), `MAX_CAPTION_BYTES = 1 024`, and from S0: `FRAGMENT_MAX_BYTES = 16 MiB` of samples per fragment (§8.3), `MAX_AUDIO_CHANNELS = 8` (§8.3), `EXPORT_STALL_MS = 30 000` without progress before an export is cancelled (§6.2); a send is refused with `STORAGE_FULL` when free space is below `3 × F × 64 KiB` (the files, the outbox window and SQLCipher headroom). A send-side refusal is local and visible to nobody else; the receive side never refuses for space (§7.5).

---

## 7. Recipient pipeline (normative; ADR-45)

### 7.1 Automatic download, and why it is the only reflex-free choice

`M_0` is a listened namespace: Phase 7 lists it on its own pair schedule and fetches every listed blob within the media fetch budget (§5.5), exactly as it fetches `X_0`. The media actor claims `Consumer.MEDIA` blobs and moves them into the ciphertext store.

- **No reflex (T28, T47).** Nothing the recipient receives, decrypts, validates or views, and no local state (the pool's occupancy, which blobs are bound, free space), changes what it asks relays for: the `get`s on `M_0` follow the stores others made into `M_0` (declared in LIMITE L2.1 #3 and F9 §11.1), never a descriptor. A descriptor withheld by a relay and one delivered produce identical normalized relay views (NI-M1), also when the pool is full (NI-M6).
- **Rejected: fetch on descriptor** (`Inbox.want`, F7:1419). It needs a "want pair" schedule the engine lacks (the fetch step runs only in a read pair's bundle, `ghost/android/sync/src/main/kotlin/org/ghost/sync/engine/WorkLane.kt:77-83`) and it is a reflex: the relay sees `X` consumed, then `M` fetched, linking the descriptor to its object and timing the consumer.
- **Rejected: fetch on tap.** The first `get` of an object tells the relay when the user looked (for view-once, when it was opened): a read receipt to the relay.
- **Cost, declared.** Every object deposited into my `M` is downloaded, bounded only by what the depositors can pay for (§5.3: the allowances of every relay of my union set, plus a hostile card holder's own tokens), including from contacts I have blocked (they keep my card) and from card holders who spam. A Phase 13 global "pause media download" is a user action that turns `M_0`'s listening off (relays then see the listening stop; L9-M3). Q4 keeps the choice with the owner.

### 7.2 Ingest, descriptors, binding (transactions on the media actor unless stated)

The ciphertext store is **content-addressed with references** (D25): one `media_blob(blob_hash, file_name, origin, retain_until_day)` row and one file per distinct hash, and `media_fragment(object_id, ordinal) → blob_hash` rows that name blobs; any number of objects may name one hash. A blob is **pooled** when no `bound` fragment row names it.

- **`MEDIA-INGEST`** (media actor): for each claimed blob (`Inbox.claim(MEDIA, 32)`): check `|blob| = 65 536` (anything else is consumed and dropped, counted); if a `media_blob` with that hash already exists (a second relay's copy, a replay), consume it without writing a file; else write it to a new random-named file, `force(true)`, sync the directory; then one transaction: `Inbox.markConsumed`, insert `media_blob(hash, file_name, origin = 'ingested', retain_until_day = min(day(E) + 1, today + 8))` with `E = InboundBlob.relayExpiryHour`, and set **every** `expected` fragment row naming that hash, of any object, to `bound`. Then apply the pool rule of §7.5 (eviction, never a stop). A crash between the file write and the commit leaves an orphan file (reconciled) and an unconsumed row (offered again); nothing is lost and no file is written twice. Until `markConsumed`, the blob's ciphertext sits in Phase 7's `inbox_blob.ciphertext` column (`Schema.kt:370-386`), which consumption sets to NULL (`InboxStore.kt:392-396`); at most `FETCHED_CAP` = 256 blobs (16 MiB) of `M_0` are there at once (§10.4, §12.4).
- **Descriptor (inside F9 `RECV` or the held replay, DM actor).** A kind-5 L0 from a contact, returned by `decrypt` or `sessionInbound` with its `key_env` (§4.2), creates the message row as F9 does and, through `MediaObjects.onDescriptor(tx, messageId, contactId, body, key_env)`, a `media_object(role = 'in', dest_id = contactId, state = 'awaiting', key_env, manifest_hash, kind, flags, class, incomplete_after_hour = relayExpiryHour of the descriptor blob)`. A second descriptor naming an existing `object_id` is `drop(duplicate_object)` for the object (the message follows F9's dedup); `object_id` never appears outside E2E encryption, so a third party cannot squat it.
- **A descriptor in a request (D28, Q36).** F9 §12.3 turns an intro from an unknown card holder into a request (`trust_state = 'request'`, `request_until_day = today + 7`) and stores its content rows like any contact's. For a kind-5 L0 in a request, `RECV` stores the message row (the caption, like a text request) but calls no `onDescriptor`: the `key_env` that function 14 returned is dropped unstored, and no `media_object` exists, so nothing on the device can open the object. Its fragments are still fetched (D10) and pooled, then dropped at their retention day. `acceptRequest` does not bring the object back; the user may ask the sender to send it again (nothing is sent automatically, F9 §12.3's no-reflex rule). **Why:** with the revision-2 rule, anyone holding my card link could leave openable media on my device for the request's seven days without my ever choosing to see it, and a seized device (AD-8, AD-12) would hold it; text in a request is a smaller version of the same exposure and stays F9's rule. A blocked contact's deposits are already dropped by F9 (§12.4), so no descriptor of theirs arrives.
- **`MEDIA-BIND`** (media actor; runs when an `awaiting` object's manifest hash has a `media_blob`, or when a manifest blob is ingested for an `awaiting` object): read the manifest file, verify its hash, call `MediaNative.readerOpen` (§13.2), which unwraps `key_env` under its binding, checks the commitment, opens the manifest, parses it strictly and compares `object_id`, `kind`, `flags`, `class_code` with the descriptor, then closes the reader (the key leaves native memory again); then in one transaction insert one `media_fragment` row per ordinal, `bound` if a `media_blob` with that hash exists and `expected` otherwise, whether or not another object names the same hash; set `state = 'fetching'`. A manifest that does not open, or disagrees with its descriptor, makes the object `corrupt` (counted; the message shows "media unavailable").
- **Ownership is decided by authentication, never by arrival order.** A card holder who can list `M` (L7-D16) and names another sender's fragment hashes in its own manifest only makes its **own** object fail in `MEDIA-VERIFY` (`AD_c` binds `object_id` and the chunk key is its own), and the honest object verifies from the same files. Revision 1's "a hash already bound to another object is `corrupt` for the newer one" let such a card holder capture fragments during a victim's upload and make the victim's object `corrupt`; it is removed.
- **Releasing references.** An object that becomes `corrupt` or `incomplete` deletes its fragment rows and sets its `key_env` to NULL in the same transaction (the checkpoint of §11.1 follows); an `ingested` blob that no row names any more returns to the pool with its original `retain_until_day` (another object's descriptor may still arrive for it), a `sealed` one is deleted. An erasure (SWEEP, view-once close, the sender's view-once cleanup) deletes the object's rows **and** every blob no row names any more, whatever its origin; a blob another live object still names stays until that object releases it (only possible when a card holder names the same hashes; declared in P-M8).
- **Completeness.** When the last `expected` row of an object is bound, the object becomes `complete` and `MEDIA-VERIFY` is queued.

### 7.3 `MEDIA-VERIFY` (the full validation pass before any exposure; RQ-M6)

On the media actor, outside a transaction: open a reader, feed every chunk in order to `readerChunk(mode = validate)`, which authenticates it, checks the pad and runs the canonical walker (§8.2, §8.3); then `readerValidateFinish`, which also compares the manifest's dimensions and duration with the walked stream (§3.5). One transaction records `verified` or `corrupt` (a `corrupt` object releases its references, §7.2). Nothing is shown before `verified`; plaintext exists only chunk by chunk in native memory and is zeroised. This pass runs for view-once objects too, so their content is decrypted (transiently, in native memory) before the view: ADR-27 point 12's "decrypted only in memory at display" is read as "nothing exposed before display" (X-M14, Q11). A file found corrupted **after** `verified` (a hash or tag failure while reading, §15.8 item 3) makes the object `corrupt` too: the reader fails with `HASH_MISMATCH` or `NOT_OPEN`, the player ends with a typed error, and one transaction records `verified → corrupt`.

### 7.4 Display

- **Previews** are decoded in the sandbox only for the conversation named by `setConversationOnScreen` while the process is in the foreground (§6.5), never in the background and never for a blocked contact (a request has no object to preview, D28), and kept in a bounded RAM cache (§10.5).
- **Images:** the reader decrypts the object into a `SharedMemory` region (≤ 1 MiB), the sandbox decodes it (§9.2), the main process wraps the returned pixels in a `Bitmap`.
- **Video:** Media3 plays the verified object through GHOST's `DataSource` (§10.2).
- **The sender's own objects** are opened the same way; the sealer's walker already validated every byte, so an `out` object in `uploading`, `uploaded` or `failed` state is readable without `MEDIA-VERIFY` (a view-once object only until the sender's cleanup, §6.4).
- **Countdown.** `markDisplayed` starts the message's recipient countdown as for text (F9 §10.3; ADR-27 point 9); opening the full media does not restart it.

### 7.5 Incomplete, expired, unmatched

- An object not `complete` by `incomplete_after_hour` (the descriptor's relay expiry; by R-MT3 every honest fragment has expired by then) becomes `incomplete` (declared loss, L9-M5); it releases its references (§7.2).
- A pooled blob past `retain_until_day` is deleted with its file: unmatched blobs live at most eight days or until their relay expiry, whichever is first. A descriptor that F9 holds undecrypted behind a key change (F9 §8.3, up to 30 d + 1 h) for more than eight days therefore finds its fragments gone and the object becomes `incomplete` (declared, L9-M5); keeping unmatched blobs longer would let a card holder pin disk space for a month.
- **`POOL_CAP` = 512 MiB of pooled files, enforced by local eviction, never by fetching less.** After every ingest transaction, while pooled bytes exceed `POOL_CAP`, the pooled blobs with the earliest `retain_until_day` (ties by hash) are deleted, rows in the transaction and files by the reconcile. `MEDIA-INGEST` **keeps claiming** and Phase 7 keeps fetching, so the `get`s on `M_0` stay a function of what others stored and never of what is pooled, bound, withheld or delivered. Revision 1 stopped claiming at the cap, which filled `FETCHED_CAP` and stopped the `get`s (F7:850); since binding frees pool space, a delivered descriptor and a withheld one then led to different fetching near the cap, a relay-visible reflex (T28, T47) that NI-M1 did not exercise; NI-M6 now does. The cost: under a flood an honest object whose descriptor arrives late can find its fragments evicted and become `incomplete` (declared, L9-M4).
- **Free-space floor.** Below `MEDIA_FREE_FLOOR` = 256 MiB of free device storage, `MEDIA-INGEST` consumes each claimed blob **without** writing a file (counted, the object may become `incomplete`), again so that local state never changes what is fetched.

### 7.6 Object states (each transition has a trigger in the schema, §13.6)

| Role | States | Driven by |
|---|---|---|
| out | `uploading` → `uploaded` / `failed`; `failed` → `uploading` (user retry before the deadline, at most 3); any → `gone` | MEDIA-COMMIT, MEDIA-FEED, MEDIA-OUTCOME, MEDIA-READY, retry, deadline, SWEEP |
| in | `awaiting` → `fetching` / `corrupt`; `fetching` → `complete`; `complete` → `verified` / `corrupt`; `awaiting`/`fetching` → `incomplete`; `verified` → `corrupt` (on-disk corruption found while reading, §7.3); `verified` → `undecodable` (two sandbox failures, §9.2); `verified` → `viewing` → `viewed` (view-once only); any → `gone` | RECV, held replay, MEDIA-BIND, MEDIA-INGEST, MEDIA-VERIFY, reader, display, VIEW-ONCE-OPEN/CLOSE, MEDIA-GC, SWEEP |

`gone` exists only inside the transaction that deletes the row; it is a trigger-checked transition name, not a stored state.

---

## 8. Canonical formats and metadata stripping (normative; ADR-46; T43 = T5 made exact)

### 8.1 Principle

Metadata is removed by **re-encoding**, never by editing the source, and the output is proved clean by **canonicalisation and validation in Rust** twice: the sealer refuses to seal a non-canonical stream on the sender, and the reader refuses to show one on the recipient. Surgical stripping is rejected because the places metadata hides are open-ended: JPEG APP segments, IFD1 thumbnails that can show the uncropped original, MPF secondary images, bytes after EOI (a Motion Photo appends a whole MP4 with its own atoms; Samsung's SEFT trailer carries a country code and depth maps), PNG text chunks, HEIF items, MP4 `udta`, `meta`/`keys` and `uuid` boxes, telemetry tracks and H.264 SEI user data. AndroidX `ExifInterface` writes only JPEG, PNG and WebP and edits only EXIF and XMP (platform-media digest, B3), so it cannot be the stripper.

### 8.2 Images: re-encode, canonicalise, validate

**In the sandbox (`STRIP_IMAGE`):** decode with `ImageDecoder.createSource(ByteBuffer)` (software allocator), a header listener that refuses more than 100 MP and sets the target size of the canonical geometry (D29: long edge 512, 1 024 or 2 048 px, the largest not above the source's long edge after orientation, 512 for smaller sources; short edge `max(16, 16 · round(long · ratio / 16))`), `setTargetColorSpace(SRGB)`; `ImageDecoder` applies the EXIF orientation (**verified in S0, V-M4:** all eight orientations, in the main process and in the isolated process, on API 29 and 35; `BitmapFactory`, the control, ignored the tag in 7 of 8), so no `ExifInterface` fallback exists; composite any alpha onto white; `setGainmap(null)` on API 34+; `Bitmap.compress(JPEG, q)` with q = 85, 80, … 60, then step down the long-edge ladder (2 048 → 1 024 → 512, so the geometry stays canonical) and repeat, until the output is ≤ 1 048 512 B. **Why a ladder (D29, Q37):** revision 2 kept every source's exact dimensions up to 2 048 px, and a screenshot's size is the screen's, which often names the phone model; with the ladder only the long-edge step and the aspect ratio rounded to 16 px leave the device.

**Canonical JPEG profile (`ghost-client-media::jpeg`).**

| Allowed | Refused |
|---|---|
| `SOI`; `DQT` (8-bit, 1–4 tables); `SOF0` only (baseline, 8-bit, 1 or 3 components, the canonical geometry of D29: for an object a long edge of 512, 1 024 or 2 048, for a preview 320, and a short edge that is a multiple of 16 not above the long edge; the walker reports them to the sealer and the reader, §3.5); `DHT`; `DRI`; `SOS`; entropy-coded data with `RST0`–`RST7`; `EOI` | every `APP0`–`APP15` (JFIF, EXIF, XMP, ICC, MPF, IPTC, JUMBF/C2PA, Adobe), `COM`, `SOF1`–`SOF15` other than `SOF0` (progressive, lossless, arithmetic), `DNL`, `DAC`, `DHP`, `EXP`, reserved markers, **any byte after EOI**, a missing EOI, a second SOI |

- `jpegCanonicalize` (sender, over GHOST's own encoder output only) drops `APP0`–`APP15` and `COM`, keeps the allowed segments byte for byte, and fails on anything in the refused column; the security property comes from the re-encode (only pixels cross), the profile makes it checkable. **Measured in S0 (V-M6, API 29 and 35):** `Bitmap.compress(JPEG)` always writes baseline `SOF0` with `APP0` JFIF and an `APP2` ICC profile, also for sRGB (474 B on API 35, 554 B on API 29), which the canonicaliser drops, so a canonical file carries no profile and STRIP's conversion to sRGB is what keeps the colours right. A bitmap with a gain map compresses to an Ultra HDR file with a second JPEG after `EOI` (982–995 B), which the canonicaliser refuses; converting to sRGB does not drop the gain map, `setGainmap(null)` does, and `STRIP_IMAGE` as written left no trailer. Compressing the same pixels gives byte-identical canonical files on both levels, but STRIP outputs of one source differ by up to about 3 % between levels (the decoder's scaling), so the JPEG goldens are per API level. ExifTool finds 0 tags outside the structural allowlist and 0 canaries in every canonical output.
- The recipient's reader requires `canonicalize(x) = x` for the object and for the preview: a sender with a modified client cannot deliver a JPEG outside the profile to a decoder.

### 8.3 Video: transcode, GhostMuxer, sealed stream

**Transformer configuration (corrected by S0: S0-3, S0-13, S0-18, S0-20, S0-21, S0-22).** `Transformer.Builder(context)` with `setVideoMimeType(VIDEO_H264)`, `setAudioMimeType(AUDIO_AAC)` and `setPortraitEncodingEnabled(false)`, set explicitly; one `EditedMediaItemSequence` built from the item with **`experimentalSetForceAudioTrack(true)`**, so that a source without audio still leaves with a silent AAC track and every canonical video has exactly one audio and one video track (S0-3; 1.11.1's Composition-level flag threw for a sequence built from items on API 35, while the sequence's works: measured on API 35 and 29); video effects `Presentation.createForWidthAndHeight(1280, 720, LAYOUT_SCALE_TO_FIT)` for a source displayed landscape and `(720, 1280, …)` for one displayed portrait (an output frame of exactly that size with the source fitted inside and empty bars added, Presentation's layout documentation) and `FrameDropEffect.createDefaultFrameDropEffect(30)` (15 on the retry of §6.2); audio processors `SonicAudioProcessor` with `setOutputSampleRateHz(48 000)` and a `ChannelMixingAudioProcessor` with **an explicit matrix for every input count 1 … 8** (S0-20: `ChannelMixingMatrix.createForConstantGain(n, 2)` throws for every n from 3 to 8 in 1.11.1; 5.1 as ITU-R BS.775 without LFE, other counts alternating left/right at gain 2/n; the matrices are constants of `GhostTranscoder` with a unit test, and more than 8 input channels are refused, `UNSUPPORTED_SOURCE`); the forcing encoder factory below around `DefaultEncoderFactory` with requested `VideoEncoderSettings`: 2 000 000 bit/s, **`BITRATE_MODE_VBR`** (S0-18: on API 35's encoder CBR overshot more than VBR on hard content and API 29's has no CBR; CBR only on an encoder that S0b measures better in CBR), **`setEncodingProfileLevel(AVCProfileBaseline, AVCLevel31)`, `setMaxBFrames(0)` and `setiFrameIntervalSeconds(1f)`** (S0-22), and 128 kbit/s AAC-LC; `setUsePlatformDiagnostics(false)` (Media3 `Transformer.Builder`); a `DefaultAssetLoaderFactory` that reads the source through `ContentDataSource` and parses it with **`Mp4Extractor` only**, with a `BitmapLoader` that refuses every type and no image duration (S0-21: with Media3's `DefaultExtractorsFactory`, which the one-argument `ProgressiveMediaSource.Factory` uses, a WebM file was transcoded, while with `Mp4Extractor` alone PNG, JPEG, WebP, HEIF, AVIF and WebM all fail with a typed error; measured on API 35 and 29); and `setMuxerFactory(GhostMuxer.Factory)`. **Correction:** revision 2 said a `Presentation` capped the frame rate at 30 fps; `Presentation` controls only size, aspect ratio and layout (its source, read 2026-09-25), so a 60 fps source kept its rate; the frame-drop effect is what caps it. A source below 30 fps keeps its rate (frames are not duplicated; declared, L9-M9). **Canonical parameters (D29, Q37):** every video leaves as 1 280 × 720 or 720 × 1 280 displayed, SDR, at most 30 fps, with 48 kHz stereo AAC-LC, whatever the source's dimensions, frame rate, sample rate and channel count, which otherwise identify the recording device. **Verified in S0 (V-M24, API 29 and 35)** once the corrections of this section are applied: 1 280 × 720 from 1 920 × 1 080, 640 × 480 (bars) and 1 280 × 720; every sample duration 3 000 (30.000 fps) from a 60-fps source; 24 fps kept; 48 kHz stereo from 22.05 and 44.1 kHz, mono and 5.1. **A portrait source is coded as 1 280 × 720 with a 90° or 270° `tkhd` matrix** (portrait encoding off, set explicitly: with it on, API 29's encoder produced 612 × 1 088 through Transformer's encoder fallback, a non-canonical size; S0-20), so the coded size is always 1 280 × 720 and the displayed geometry of §3.5 is the coded size turned by the matrix. **The rotation passes through (S0, review I19):** `VideoEncoderWrapper` keeps the source's rotation whenever its axis matches the output's, so a 270° source leaves with 270° and a 180° source with 180° (measured on API 35 and 29; its bytecode), and the recipient learns the source's rotation, which usually tells the front camera from the back one; declared (L9-M9); Q45 offers normalising it to 0° or 90°.

**Encoding is forced (D15).** These settings alone do **not** force a transcode: "If the input media format already matches the configurations for audio or video, Transformer automatically switches to transmuxing" (Media3 Transformer documentation, Transformations page). In `TransformerUtil.shouldTranscodeVideo`, a single-item composition whose encoder factory does not require encoding, with HDR mode "keep", the requested MIME type equal to the input's, square pixels, default speed and only no-op or rotation effects (a `Presentation` whose output size equals the input size is a no-op) is transmuxed; `shouldTranscodeAudio` transmuxes AAC input when no audio processor is set. A phone video that is already H.264 + AAC at ≤ 720p would then reach the recipient as the camera's own bitstream: its SPS/VUI encoder fingerprint, its original bitrate (breaking the `VIDEO_MAX_MS` budget) and any AAC data-stream or fill elements. So:
1. `GhostTranscoder`'s `Codec.EncoderFactory` overrides `videoNeedsEncoding()` and `audioNeedsEncoding()` to return `true` (both default to `false`; `Codec.java`), which makes both `shouldTranscode…` functions return `true` first;
2. HDR sources are tone-mapped to SDR (the Composition HDR mode, not "keep HDR"), so the output is always 8-bit SDR H.264; S0 could not exercise it (no HDR source or 10-bit encoder on the emulators: an S0b checklist line on a phone that records HDR);
3. before `sealFinish`, the export must name its encoders (`ExportResult.videoEncoderName` and `audioEncoderName` non-null; with the forced silent track every output has audio; each is "null if none were used", `ExportResult.java`; for the generated silent track Media3 reports `audioConversionProcess` as transmuxed while naming the AAC encoder, measured, so the check reads the names, not the conversion process), or the preparation is aborted and its orphans reconciled (§6.2). **Verified in S0 (V-M17, API 29 and 35):** with the forcing factory both names are set and no source NAL unit, AAC frame or canary survives (0 identical NAL units or AAC frames; the transmux control carried every source unit and the DSE canary); Media3 1.11.1 also forces encoding by itself once settings are requested, so the wrapper and the name check guard against a future version;
4. the T5 corpus includes sources that already match the output (H.264 + AAC, ≤ 720p30, SDR, square pixels) with distinctive SPS/VUI values and a canary in an AAC data-stream element (§15.7), and mutant PM-M18 ("transmux allowed") must be caught. Transformer's `start(…, path)` needs a path; `GhostMuxer.Factory.create(path)` ignores it. The Transformer class itself creates, deletes and checks no file (its source, read 2026-09-25), and no component it drives wrote one in S0 (V-M1, 96 exports on API 29 and 35 under a file inventory, `FileObserver` on every app directory and a root search of `/data`);
5. **the encoder's own settings are GHOST's, not Media3's or the source's (S0-22):** (a) **profile and level:** on API 29+ Media3 1.11.1's `DefaultEncoderFactory.adjustMediaFormatForH264EncoderSettings` sets the AVC profile to High whenever the encoder supports High, overwriting a requested profile, and keeps a requested level (bytecode, S0); so phones with a High-capable encoder write **High profile at level 3.1** and Baseline-only encoders (the emulators') Baseline at level 3.1 (measured: `level_idc` 31 with the request, 41 without, on API 35). The walker accepts both (below); Q44 offers GHOST's own encoder format for Baseline everywhere. (b) **B-frames:** `max-bframes` 0 is set explicitly (the platform's default is 0, Media3 sets the key only when asked; a B-frame would need composition offsets, which the profile refuses). (c) **frame rate:** Transformer configures the encoder with the **source's** frame rate, before `FrameDropEffect` drops frames, so a 60-fps source got key frames every 2 s of output and level 3.2 (S0, API 35): both reveal a source above 30 fps. The forcing factory gives the encoder `min(source rate, 30)` instead, or 15 on the retry of §6.2 (measured on API 35: key frames every 1 s and level 3.1; API 29's encoder showed neither effect, one IDR and level 3.1 either way). (d) **colour (S0-13, revised in the review pass):** Transformer otherwise carries the source's colour description into the VUI and the `colr` box (a BT.601 full-range source left labelled BT.601 full range on API 35, and on API 29 a `colr` box saying full range over limited-range pixels), so the forcing factory sets `Format.colorInfo` on the format it passes to `DefaultEncoderFactory`. **Which matrix the pixels are coded with is the encoder's choice, not the label's** (S0, 2026-10-04: a colour grid decoded into buffers, every patch compared with both conversions): API 35's `c2.android.avc.encoder` coded what was requested (BT.709 limited for a BT.709 request, BT.601 limited for a BT.601 request, each within 2–4 levels per channel), while API 29's `OMX.google.h264.encoder` coded BT.601 limited whatever was requested (the request changed only the `colr` box; the coded stream was byte-identical), so revision 3's fixed BT.709 label was wrong there by up to 41 levels per channel (review H4 suspected a relabel). **Rule (Q48):** the forcing factory requests **SDR BT.601 limited range** (written by Media3 as `colr` (5, 1, 6), and by API 35's encoder as the same VUI values), which both measured encoders code as labelled; and since a phone's encoder may code another matrix whatever it is asked, `GhostTranscoder` **calibrates** each encoder once per process before its first video export: it transcodes a 1-s colour-grid clip shipped with the app (a few kilobytes) through the same configuration, decodes the first output frame into a buffer and compares every patch with the BT.601 and the BT.709 conversions; it keeps the BT.601 request if that matches within 8 levels per channel, switches to a BT.709 limited request (`colr` (1, 1, 1)) if the encoder coded BT.709, and refuses video on that device if neither matches (`UNSUPPORTED_SOURCE`, detail `COLOUR_UNVERIFIED`, counted). The cost is one short extra transcode per process [I] (S5 measures it). The label then describes the pixels, and the preview's conversion (§6.2) and the recipient's player both read it; which label an encoder needs is one more device-class bit (L9-M9).

**`GhostMuxer`** implements `androidx.media3.muxer.Muxer` around `FragmentedMp4Muxer.Builder(WritableByteChannel)` (the `Mp4Muxer` alternative needs a `SeekableMuxerOutput` and seeks back to patch the `mdat` size and to rewrite `moov` near the end, `Mp4Writer`, so it cannot feed a one-pass sealer; `FragmentedMp4Writer` is strictly sequential):
1. **No metadata passes.** `addMetadataEntry` forwards nothing. `GhostMuxer` itself adds one `Mp4TimestampData` with constant creation and modification times at construction (Media3's `MetadataCollector` otherwise stamps `System.currentTimeMillis()`), and, in `addTrack` for the video track, one `Mp4OrientationData(format.rotationDegrees)` when that is 0, 90, 180 or 270 (anything else fails the export), which is what Media3's own `InAppFragmentedMp4Muxer.addTrack` does. **Corrected by S0 (V-M24, S0-20):** Transformer passes the rotation only as `Format.rotationDegrees` and never offers an `Mp4OrientationData`, so revision 3's "forwards nothing except `Mp4OrientationData`" made every portrait video play sideways. Media3 writes the orientation into **both** tracks' `tkhd` (measured). Location (`Mp4LocationData`), the source's `Mp4TimestampData`, `XmpData`, `ThumbnailMetadata` and every `MdtaMetadataEntry` (S0 saw `com.android.version`, the planted location and times offered for every source) are dropped, which is exactly what Media3's own `InAppMp4Muxer` would have forwarded (`MuxerUtil.isMetadataSupported`, platform-media digest B2).
2. **Only video and audio tracks.** Any other track the asset loader offers is refused.
3. **SEI filter.** Each H.264 sample is split into NAL units; SEI NAL units are kept only if every payload type is buffering period (0), picture timing (1) or recovery point (6); user-data SEI (payload types 4 and 5, where encoders write version strings) and any other are dropped before the sample reaches the muxer. V-M12 measures what reference encoders emit (S0: the emulators' software encoders emit no SEI at all; the filter dropped all 360 user-data SEI of a transmuxed source).
4. **Fragments as `FragmentedMp4Writer` 1.11.1 cuts them (corrected by S0, S0-2):** a fragment closes at the first video sync sample that arrives once the pending video samples span ≥ 2 s from first to last presentation time, i.e. at the first key frame at least 2 s plus one frame after the fragment's first sample: 3 s at the 1-s key-frame interval of point 5 (revision 3 said 2 s). Every fragment starts with a sync sample (every S0 output). An encoder that ignores the requested interval makes fragments as long as its own IDR spacing: API 29's `OMX.google.h264.encoder` writes an IDR every 1 000 frames whatever is requested (AOSP `SoftAVCEnc`'s `DEFAULT_IDR_INTERVAL`; the requested interval sets only non-IDR I frames), i.e. fragments of 33.3 s at 30 fps and 41.7 s at 24 fps.
5. **Fragment bound (S0-2, review I7).** `FragmentedMp4Writer` holds a whole fragment in memory until the next key frame (S0: the app's `VmHWM` rose by 39 MB while a 21 MB fragment was pending), and the sealing channel sees a fragment only after that. `GhostMuxer` therefore mirrors the writer's flush rule on the samples it is given (each sample's size and sync flag, before the writer buffers it) and fails the export as soon as the samples of one fragment, both tracks, exceed **16 MiB** (`UNSUPPORTED_SOURCE`, detail `FRAGMENT_TOO_LARGE`, §6.5); the walker refuses a `moof` + `mdat` above 16 MiB as the second line. There is **no duration bound**: the 40 s first proposed in S0 rested on an unmeasured average spacing and would have rejected API 29's own 24-fps exports (41.7-s fragments). 16 MiB is about 63 s at the profile's rate, so an encoder with 1 000-frame IDRs fails only for sources below about 16 fps (Q46). `GhostMuxer` also enforces the class cap and records the retry projection of §6.2 on the same samples, for the same reason (the sealed bytes lag by up to a fragment).

**`SealingChannel`** buffers bytes into 262 128-byte chunks and calls `sealAppend`; the native sealer runs the **fMP4 walker** on every chunk and, at `sealFinish`, appends a GHOST-built `mfra` box, because `FragmentedMp4Muxer` writes no `sidx`, `mfra` or `tfra`, and Media3's `FragmentedMp4Extractor` without them outputs `SeekMap.Unseekable` unless `FLAG_READ_MFRA_FOR_SEEK_MAP` is set and an `mfra` exists (its source, read 2026-09-25). **Corrected by S0 (V-M2, S0-2): `FragmentedMp4Muxer` 1.11.1 writes no `tfdt` either** (no class of `media3-muxer` contains the string), so revision 3's `mfra` ("time = that `moof`'s `tfdt`") had 0 entries, and an `mfra` built from summed durations without `tfdt` made the extractor stamp the samples after a seek with a stale decode time: the player showed pictures up to 91 frames away from the target with correct-looking timestamps. With `tfdt` inserted every seek landed within one frame (API 35, by content; API 29, by timestamp). So, before any byte reaches `sealAppend`, `GhostMuxer`'s channel inserts after the `tfhd` of every `traf` a version-1 `tfdt` whose value is the running sum of that track's `trun` sample durations in its `mdhd` timescale, adds 20 B per inserted box to the `moof` and `traf` sizes and to every `trun` `data_offset` of that `moof`, and rewrites each `tfhd` `base_data_offset` (absolute, flag 0x1) by the bytes inserted before it; it buffers one `moof` at a time (≤ 8 KiB at 3-s fragments, growing by 12 B per sample with the fragment) and passes `mdat` through. `sealFinish` appends an `mfra` whose `tfra` (version 1, video track only, traf/trun/sample number 1) has one entry per `moof` with time = that `moof`'s video `tfdt` and offset = its offset in the sealed stream, then `mfro`. The fMP4 carries no duration (`mvhd`/`tkhd`/`mdhd` 0, no `mehd`) and Media3's `MfraSeekMap` reports none, so `MediaHandle` exposes the manifest's `duration_ms`, and the UI and the seek tests use it (§10.2, §15.8 item 3).

**Canonical fMP4 profile (`ghost-client-media::fmp4`; written out from the S0 goldens, S0-3, S0-13, S0-20, S0-22).** Top level exactly `ftyp` (major `isom`, minor 131 072, compatible `isom iso2 mp41`), `moov`, then (`moof`, `mdat`) pairs, then GHOST's `mfra`; `moov` = `mvhd` (v0, times 0, duration 0, timescale 10 000), one `soun` and one `vide` `trak` in either order with track IDs 1 and 2 (Transformer adds audio first), and `mvex` with one `trex` per track (defaults 1, 0, 0, 0); each `trak` = `tkhd` (v0, flags 7, times and duration 0, the matrix of 0°, 90°, 180° or 270°, **the same in both tracks**), `mdia` (`mdhd` v0 with timescale 48 000 or 90 000, times and duration 0; `hdlr` `soun` "SoundHandle" or `vide` "VideoHandle"; `minf` with `smhd`/`vmhd`, `dinf`/`dref` (one self-reference), `stbl` with `stsd`, empty `stts`, `stsz`, `stsc`, `stco`, and `stss` for video only); sample entries `mp4a` + `esds` (AAC-LC, 48 kHz, 2 channels: ASC `0x1190`) and `avc1` (coded 1 280 × 720) + `avcC` (4-byte NAL lengths, one SPS, one PPS) + `pasp` (65 536:65 536) + `colr` `nclx` with full_range_flag 0 and either (5, 1, 6) (BT.601, the default request) or (1, 1, 1) (BT.709, for an encoder the calibration of point 5(d) found coding BT.709); the SPS is profile 66 (Baseline) or 100 (High: `chroma_format_idc` 1, 8-bit), `level_idc` 31, `frame_mbs_only_flag` 1, walked in full (scaling lists included) to its VUI, whose colour description is absent or exactly the `colr` values, with `video_full_range_flag` 0; each `moof` = `mfhd` (sequence 1, 2, …) + one `traf` per track = `tfhd` (flags 0x1, `base_data_offset` = the `moof`'s offset) + `tfdt` (version 1, the running sum of the track's previous `trun` durations) + `trun` (flags 0x701: data offset, per-sample duration, size and flags; no composition offsets, so no B-frames) with its data inside the following `mdat`, the first video sample of every fragment a sync sample, and one fragment's `moof` + `mdat` at most 16 MiB; `mfra` = `tfra` (version 1, the video track, one entry per `moof` with the `moof`'s video `tfdt` and offset) + `mfro`. AVC samples contain only NAL types 1, 5, 6 (allowed payloads), 7, 8 and 9; the video track's summed `trun` durations give the duration (§3.5). **Nothing else**: `edts`, `udta`, `meta`, `ilst`, `keys`, `uuid`, `free`, `skip`, `sidx`, `mehd` are refused (none appears in any S0 output). The 30 fps cap is checked by the goldens and the T43 corpus (a 60 fps source), not by the walker (the frame-drop effect keeps the average, not each interval, near 30). Anything else is `NON_CANONICAL`: the sender cannot seal it and the recipient does not play it. **The goldens** are in `ghost/protocol/test-vectors/media-goldens/media3-1.11.1/` (recorded in S0 on the API 29 and 35 emulators with this configuration, `tfdt` and `mfra` included, each checked by the S0 spike's profile checker; the S0b device goldens join them, S0-22); a Media3 bump re-records them (G-M8).

**AAC is outside the walker's reach (declared; Q35).** The walker checks the AAC track's sample entry (`mp4a` with an `esds` naming AAC-LC, 48 kHz, two channels, D29) and the sample sizes and offsets, but not the raw data blocks: finding element boundaries inside an AAC frame means Huffman-decoding the spectral data of every channel element, a codec, not a parser. AAC's data-stream (`ID_DSE`) and fill (`ID_FIL`) elements can carry arbitrary bytes. For the honest pipeline this does not matter: audio is always re-encoded (forced encoding above), so no source byte reaches it, and V-M12 records whether the reference encoders emit DSE or non-zero FIL payloads. A modified sender can put bytes there; they reach only the recipient's out-of-process AAC decoder, which ignores them, and never a metadata parser. P-M6 states the limit.

### 8.4 Why the defaults are not used (facts read in Media3 1.11.1 sources, 2026-09-25)

- `InAppMp4Muxer` (Transformer's default) opens `new FileOutputStream(path)`: a plaintext file by construction.
- `BoxParser` reads `©xyz` into `Mp4LocationData` and `mvhd` into `Mp4TimestampData`; `MuxerWrapper` forwards every `Format.metadata` entry; the MP4 muxers accept location, timestamps, XMP and string or float `mdta` entries. A default transcode of a phone video therefore carries its GPS, creation time and `com.android.*` keys into the output (platform-media digest B2; AOSP `MPEG4Writer` writes `com.android.version`, optionally manufacturer and model, capture fps and `©xyz`).
- Media3's `AesCipherDataSource` is `AES/CTR/NoPadding` (`AesFlushingCipher`): unauthenticated, so it cannot satisfy step 7.

### 8.5 What survives, by design (declared; L9-M9, L9-M10)

The correspondent receives pixels and sound. Re-encoding does not remove the camera sensor's noise pattern (PRNU), which can link photos to one camera; the sender's video encoder leaves stream parameters (SPS/PPS/VUI) typical of a device class (GHOST pins the level, the B-frames, the key-frame interval, the frame rate and the colour, but Media3 picks High profile on High-capable encoders, S0-22); the source's rotation passes through (S0, Q45); duration, the aspect ratio (rounded to 16 px in images, as bars in video), the image's long-edge step and a source frame rate below 30 fps reach the recipient, while exact dimensions, a higher frame rate, the sample rate and the channel count do not (D29); C2PA provenance, where the source had it, is removed with the rest. Watermarking is P2 (LIMITE:138).

---

## 9. Decoding attack surface and isolation (normative; ADR-46; T46)

### 9.1 Surfaces

| Surface | Who controls the bytes | Where it runs | Control |
|---|---|---|---|
| Received image and preview | any contact, a modified client | **isolated process** (platform `ImageDecoder`, libjpeg-turbo in Skia) | canonical JPEG only, validated before the sandbox sees it; the canonical geometry (long edge ≤ 2 048 for an object, 320 for a preview, D29); one request per process instance |
| Picked source image (user's own file, possibly downloaded from anywhere) | whoever made the file | **isolated process** | magic-byte allowlist first (JPEG, PNG, static WebP; HEIC/HEIF/AVIF refused, S0-4); 64 MiB and 100 MP limits |
| Received video container | any contact | main process: Media3 `FragmentedMp4Extractor` (Java, memory-safe) | canonical fMP4, validated end to end before playback; only that extractor is registered |
| Received video bitstream | any contact | `MediaCodec`: the codecs run in the platform's media processes, not in the app; since Android 10 software codecs run in a constrained process with tightened SELinux and seccomp (Android Developers blog, 2019-05) | canonical fMP4; only H.264 and AAC |
| Picked source video | the file's author | main process: Media3's `Mp4Extractor` alone (Java; S0-21); codecs out of process | `sniff` refuses non-ISO-BMFF and HEIF/AVIF brands first; Transformer's typed errors |

The record motivates the isolation: libwebp CVE-2023-4863 was exploited in the wild; the LANDFALL spyware used a malformed DNG against Samsung's `libimagecodec.quram.so` (CVE-2025-21042) through a messaging app; the Android bulletin of 2026-09 lists a Critical remote-code-execution fix in the System component's media codecs (CVE-2026-28618, no user interaction needed) and a High remote-code-execution fix in the Framework (CVE-2026-28609). Platform decoders are patched by monthly updates and Mainline, which de-Googled or unpatched devices may not receive; the app cannot fix them, only confine them.

### 9.2 The isolated decoding service

- **Manifest:** `<service android:name="org.ghost.media.sandbox.MediaDecodeService" android:isolatedProcess="true" android:process=":mdec" android:exported="false">` with no intent filter. An isolated service "runs under a special process that is isolated from the rest of the system and has no permissions of its own" (Android `<service>` reference): no network (no INTERNET), no access to GHOST's files, database or Keystore.
- **One process instance per request (D17, Q25).** The main process binds with `Context.bindIsolatedService(intent, BIND_AUTO_CREATE, instanceName, executor, connection)` (API 29 = minSdk) and a **fresh random `instanceName` per request**: without `BIND_SHARED_ISOLATED_PROCESS`, each instance name gets its own isolated process. After the reply (or a failure) it unbinds, and the instance's process dies, taking decoded plaintext with it. A `STRIP_IMAGE` and a `DECODE_IMAGE` never share an instance, and neither do two objects (an image's preview and its full decode are two requests). Lock and wipe unbind every instance at once. At most `MAX_DECODER_PROCESSES = 2` instances live at a time. **Why:** revision 1 kept one `:mdec` process for 30 s after the last request and served both operations from it; an exploit delivered by one image (received, or a file the user picked) then stayed resident, saw every later decoded image from every contact, and produced the "re-encoded" JPEGs of the user's next sends, where it could hide other contacts' pixels in entropy-coded data that still passes the marker-level canonicaliser, for GHOST's own send path to deliver. With one request per process, an exploit sees only the bytes of its own request. **The cost is one isolated-process start per request, measured in S0 (V-M18): 100 of 100 requests got distinct processes that died within milliseconds of the unbind on API 29 and 35; bind + 320-px decode + reply took 0.16–2.5 s at the median on the loaded emulators across three sessions (the bind is 89–96 % of it, outliers to 7 s), which is not a device figure. Criterion (Q40):** on the reference mid-range phone (S0b), the p90 of bind + 320-px decode + reply must be ≤ 250 ms, so that two instances fill a screen of eight previews in about one second. Above it, the first fallback is **one pre-bound spare instance**: while a conversation is on screen, the client keeps one fresh, unused instance bound and hands the next request to it, binding a new spare at once; each instance still serves exactly one request, so the isolation property is unchanged and only the bind latency is hidden (S0 review I14; the spare counts against `MAX_DECODER_PROCESSES`, and none is bound in the background). Only if that is still too slow does Q25's fallback batch the previews of one sender on one screen into one instance (weaker, declared). Previews show a placeholder until decoded. `STRIP_IMAGE` and full decodes never use a shared instance.
- **The Application does nothing in the isolated process (V-M19).** Android instantiates the app's `Application` in every process, including isolated ones; `GhostApp.onCreate` (`GhostApp.kt:146-158`) runs `wiring.onProcessStart()` and `messagingWiring.onProcessStart(keys.exists())`, which open no-backup files, the Keystore and system services an isolated process may not touch, so `:mdec` would fail on every bind. `GhostApp.onCreate` therefore returns right after `super.onCreate()` when `Process.isIsolated()` (API 28+), before any lazy field is touched; `MediaDecodeService` uses only the platform decoder APIs. An emulator test binds the service on API 29, 35 and 37 and decodes a canonical JPEG (T46 suite), and mutant MC-M23 (the early return removed) must fail it. **Verified in S0 (V-M19, API 29 and 35):** the isolated process runs the `Application` like any other process (no content provider of the default process is created there), every GhostApp-like operation but a bare Keystore load fails inside it, and without the early return a bind never connects (no `onServiceConnected`, no `onBindingDied` within 15 s), so MC-M23 shows as a request timeout, not as a crash the client can see. The binding test therefore asserts that the guarded bind connects before this section's 10-s request timeout and that the mutant's does not; it asserts no speed (S0 measured single guarded binds of 0.1–3.4 s on loaded emulators; corrected by S0-15, which first proposed a 2-s threshold).
- **Protocol.** A Binder interface with exactly two operations, inputs and outputs in `SharedMemory` regions the main process creates, maps and unmaps: `STRIP_IMAGE(encoded source, maxEdge, maxBytes) → encoded JPEG` (§8.2) and `DECODE_IMAGE(canonical JPEG, maxW, maxH) → ARGB_8888 pixels, w, h`. Input regions are set read-only (`SharedMemory.setProtect(PROT_READ)`) before they are passed. Type sniffing is not the sandbox's job: `MediaNative.sniff` (Rust, main process) reads the first 64 bytes and refuses anything outside the source allowlist before the sandbox sees a byte. The main process checks every answer **on its own copy, taken after the instance is unbound** (no live sandbox can change the bytes between the check and the use): `w` and `h` in range and `size = w · h · 4` for pixels, `jpegCanonicalize` for re-encoded output; it parses nothing else the sandbox returns. Every region is zero-filled before it is unmapped and closed.
- **Parcel discipline.** The interface's parcels carry only `SharedMemory` handles and ints; a malformed reply (wrong handle count, a size mismatch) is a failure, never parsed further. The sandbox has no callback into the main process.
- **Failure.** A timeout (10 s per request), a crash of the instance (`DeadObjectException`) or an out-of-range answer is a typed failure; after two failures on one object the object is `undecodable` and is never offered to the sandbox again (the F9 poison-guard idea; CS-M8). A crash leaves a tombstone the OS writes (§10.3).
- **What S0 settled (V-M5, V-M15, V-M18).** An isolated process cannot use any decoder that goes through `media.player`: the platform's HEIF and AVIF image path (`HeifDecoderImpl`) looks that service up, which the isolated-app SELinux domain may not do (API 35 refuses at once, API 29 waits until the request's timeout), so HEIC/HEIF/AVIF sources are refused (§6.1, Q39; R-M3 happened). JPEG, PNG and static WebP decode in the sandbox, from a read-only `SharedMemory` region, on API 29 and 35, and `ImageDecoder` applies all eight EXIF orientations there (V-M4). Video is decoded by `MediaCodec` in the main process's client, never in `:mdec`. **Still open (S0b):** the per-instance cost on the reference phone (Q40), and whether a `<process android:process=":mdec" android:memtagMode="sync">` entry, which AAPT2 accepts and API 35 parses, applies to isolated instances, whose process name carries an instance suffix (`…:mdec:…IsoService:<instance>`): the MTE device reads a bound instance's tagged-address state (V-M15, S0-11; Q21).

### 9.3 Zero-click policy

Nothing is decoded in the background. A preview is decoded only while its conversation is on screen; requests and blocked contacts are never decoded (§7.4). `MEDIA-VERIFY` parses containers (Rust walkers, memory-safe) and authenticates, but does not decode pixels or samples.

---

## 10. Playback and the no-plaintext-at-rest rule (normative; ADR-46; T44)

### 10.1 The rule
